(9)

for each stub
p
of the motif
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
(a stub is
a node with degree
1
in
\mathsf{a}
).
The conservation relations (
8
) and (
9
) can be used to reduce the
number of variables in the moment equations via substitution. This can result
in a substantial reduction in the number of moment equations (see Table
A2
).

VI
Truncation and substitution

When we use
(
6
) to express expected rates of change for the set
of motifs up to a chosen maximum size
k
,
we obtain a truncated hierarchy of moment equations. This linear system of differential equations has the form

\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{k}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{1}&\mathbf{Q}_{12}&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\cdots&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{Q}_{2}&\mathbf{Q}_{23}&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\mathbf{0}&\ddots&\ddots&\mathbf{0}&\vdots\\
\vdots&\cdots&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{Q}_{k-1}&\mathbf{Q}_{k-1,k}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\cdots&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{Q}_{k}&\mathbf{Q}_{k,k+1}\end{bmatrix}.\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}_{1}\\
\mathbf{x}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{x}_{k}\\
\mathbf{x}_{k+1}\\
\end{bmatrix}\;=:\;\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}&&\mathbf{x}_{1}&\mathbf{x}_{2}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{k-1}&\mathbf{x}_{k}&\mathbf{x}_{k+1}\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{1}&&\mathbf{Q}_{1}&\mathbf{Q}_{12}&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\cdots&\mathbf{0}\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{2}&&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{Q}_{2}&\mathbf{Q}_{23}&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\mathbf{0}&\ddots&\ddots&\mathbf{0}&\vdots\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{k-1}&&\vdots&\cdots&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{Q}_{k-1}&\mathbf{Q}_{k-1,k}&\mathbf{0}\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{k}&&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\cdots&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{Q}_{k}&\mathbf{Q}_{k,k+1}\\
\end{array}\;,

(10)

where we defined a more compact notation on the right.
In (
10
),
\mathbf{x}_{m}
are vectors with all dynamically relevant motif counts of size
m
,
\mathbf{Q}_{m}
the
coefficients
for
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{m}:=d\mathbf{x}_{m}/dt
with motifs of the same size, and
\mathbf{Q}_{m,m+1}
the
coefficients for
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{m}
with motifs of the size
m{+}1
. The block diagonal
form arises because the change of size-
m
motif counts depends only on motif counts of size
m
and of size
m{+}1
. In Appendix
A-II
,
(
10
) is shown for SIS spreading up to a maximum motif size of
k=3
.

The substitution via conservation relations can be written as

\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{E}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{c},

(11)

where
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{1},...,\mathbf{x}_{k}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}

is
\mathbf{x}
with the to be substituted elements omitted.
\mathbf{E}
,
\mathbf{c}

contain the coefficients of linear dependence from (
7
,
9
), with
their
i
th row/element corresponding to the identity transformation for motifs that
are not substituted. Substituting this
into (
10
) results in the system of equations for the remaining
motifs
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
:

\dot{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}=\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1\cdots k,1\cdots k}\cdot(\mathbf{E}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{c})+\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1\cdots k,k+1}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k+1}

(12)

(where the tilde omits the to be substituted rows/elements), such that we can write
the system in the same form as (
10
), but now with an added
constant vector:

\dot{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}=\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\\
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k+1}\\
\end{bmatrix}+\tilde{\mathbf{c}}.

(13)

For an example, see Section
A.2
.

VII
Closure scheme

Because in (
13
), counts for the largest motifs
\mathbf{x}_{k+1}
appear
on the right-hand side but not on the left-hand side, (
13
) is underdetermined.
A closure scheme provides a way of expressing the
undetermined parts
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k+1}
in (
10
)
through a nonlinear function
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k+1}\approx\tilde{\mathbf{f}}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}})
, creating
a closed system of ODEs:

\dot{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}=\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\\
\tilde{\mathbf{f}}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}})\\
\end{bmatrix}+\tilde{\mathbf{c}},

(14)

where
\tilde{\mathbf{f}}
also depends on counts of induced subgraphs of order up to
k{+}1

(
[g_{1}^{k+1}]
in Figure
1
) if the motifs were not normalised in advance.
In this section, we develop a closure scheme that decomposes
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k+1}

into its smaller-sized components.
Our final formula generalises closures hitherto most commonly used, as shown in e.g.
House et al. 2009
.
We will show that the decomposition
is valid when: (
i
) counts of components are conditionally independent given the node states in their intersection and the adjacency
structure between them is a tree,
(
ii
) the network is spatially homogeneous, and (
iii
) the network is sufficiently large, such that the law of large numbers applies. We start with some introducing examples in
Section
A
and defer detailed explanation to Sections
B
,
C
. Examples are given in Sections
D
and
A-VIII
.

A
Introduction

When truncating the moment hierarchy at a chosen order
k
, we approximate
the order
k{+}1
motifs appearing in the equations for order
k
motifs in terms of
lower-order motifs. E.g., looking at the moment equations for SIS spreading in
Appendix
A-II
, when truncating at
k{=}1
, we would need
an expression of
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]
in terms of
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]
and
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]
. When assuming
statistical independence of neighbouring node states, the resulting expression
is of the form
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]\propto[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}][\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]
(ignoring proportionality constants for now).
Similarly, for
truncation at
k{=}2
, we would need an expression for all the 3-chains and triangles
on the right-hand side of (
A17
,
A18
). For instance,
the 3-chain
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]
is typically decomposed as
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]\propto[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}][\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]/[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]
.
Using the shorthand
x_{i}
for the event
X_{i}{=}x
, this is justified when there is a conditional independence relation of the form
P(S_{i},S_{j},I_{k}){=}P(S_{i},S_{j})P(S_{j},I_{k}{\mid}S_{j})
and if
the component probabilities are the same everywhere in the network, such that node indices

i,j,k
do not matter. This can be generalised to larger chains, such as e.g.

[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSII}}}]\propto[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}][\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SII}}}]/[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]
, which similarly follows from
the assumed conditional independence relation

P(S_{i},S_{j},I_{k},I_{l}){=}P(S_{i},S_{j},I_{k})P(S_{j},I_{k},I_{l}{\mid}S_{j},I_{k})
and homogeneity
in the network.

It is possible to generalise the
examples above to larger subgraphs by starting from the chain rule of probability,

P(\boldsymbol{x})=P(\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{n}}\mid\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{n-1}},...,\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{2}},\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{1}})...P(\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{2}}\mid\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{1}})P(\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{1}}),

where
\boldsymbol{x}
is a vector of state labels on a given network
motif, and subsequently simplify with assumed conditional independence
relations. For instance, for our second example above,
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]
, we have
P(S_{i},S_{j},I_{k}){=}P(I_{k}{\mid}S_{i},S_{j})P(S_{j}{\mid}S_{i})P(S_{i})
. If now node
k
is conditionally independent of node
i
,
we substitute
P(I_{k}{\mid}S_{i},S_{j}){=}P(I_{k}{\mid}S_{j})
, such that we obtain
the expression found above. In general, a simplification of the chain
rule in terms of subgraphs is possible if we can order the chosen
sets of subgraphs (with node indices
\boldsymbol{i}_{1},...,\boldsymbol{i}_{n}
)
without creating cycles
and if the states of adjacent subgraphs are conditionally independent given
their shared nodes. To make this precise, we need the concept of
independence map. The
independence map is a graph in which link absence
between two nodes means that there is no direct dependence between them.
For instance, if in the four-node
graph shown in row 4 column 1 of Table
1
there is statistical independence
between node states that are further than two steps removed, its independence map
is the graph shown in row 4 column 3 of Table
1
. The condition mentioned
above for simplification of the chain rule in terms of subgraphs reduces
to the requirement that the independence map be chordal, because this allows
a tree composition in terms of maximal cliques, as shown in column 4 of
Table
1
. A graph is chordal if every
cycle greater than three is cut short by a link between two non-consecutive
nodes of the cycle.

Table 1:
Examples of our method to obtain closure formulas. Because
the closures can be written independent of the state labels, the
decomposition is shown for node-indexed graphs without reference to particular
node states.

In reality, we do not know the dependence structure between
the graph nodes. However, the practical requirement of truncation of the moment hierarchy
obliges us to assume an independence map for each of the largest motifs. In order to
obtain a consistent decomposition method, we will choose as independence
map the graph obtained by connecting all
nodes to other nodes in their
d{-}1
neighbourhood, where
d
is the
diameter of the motif. The chain rule then leads to a decomposition in
terms of (maximal) cliques of the independence map in the numerator and the node
sets that separate them in the denominator. Table
1
shows
example graphs, with their diameter,
their assumed independence maps, their tree decomposition and the resulting
closure formula. The choice of dependence within a distance
d
may
in some cases result in non-chordal
independence maps, such that the decomposition cannot be made. In the
next sections, we will explain our method in detail and further show
how one can treat motifs with a non-chordal independence map.

B
Definitions and background

We will rely on the theory of decomposable Markov networks, following mostly the terminology of
Pearl 1988
.
We generalise the decomposition from a factorisation involving
(1-)cliques to one involving
d
-cliques. he definitions in this section apply to a general
graph
{\cal G}
with nodes states
X
, but we will apply the decomposition to motifs in Section
C
.

Separation

Given a graph
{\cal G}({\cal V},{\cal E})
and three disjoint
subsets of nodes

\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j},\boldsymbol{k}\subset{\cal V}
,
\boldsymbol{k}

separates

\boldsymbol{i}
and
\boldsymbol{j}
in
{\cal G}
, written as

\boldsymbol{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}_{\mkern-4.0mu{\cal G}}\;\boldsymbol{j}\mid\boldsymbol{k}
, if every
path between
\boldsymbol{i}
and
\boldsymbol{j}
has at least one vertex in
\boldsymbol{k}
.
Here,
\boldsymbol{k}
is called a separator, or also, a node cut set of
\boldsymbol{i}

and
\boldsymbol{j}
in
{\cal G}
.

Independence map

An independence map
{\cal M}
is a graph that represents
the independence between components of a set of random variables
X
such that separation
in
{\cal M}
guarantees conditional independence between corresponding subsets of
X
.
More precisely, given three disjoint subsets of nodes

\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j},\boldsymbol{k}\subset{\cal V}
,
X
possesses
a spatial Markov property:

\boldsymbol{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}_{\mkern-4.0mu{\cal M}}\;\boldsymbol{j}\mid\boldsymbol{k}\implies X_{\boldsymbol{i}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{\boldsymbol{j}}\mid X_{\boldsymbol{k}},

(15)

where the
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}
notation on the right refers to independence of the random variables:
P(X_{\boldsymbol{i}}{=}x_{\boldsymbol{i}},X_{\boldsymbol{j}}{=}x_{\boldsymbol{j}}{\mid}X_{\boldsymbol{k}}{=}x_{\boldsymbol{k}})=P(X_{\boldsymbol{i}}{=}x_{\boldsymbol{i}}{\mid}X_{\boldsymbol{k}}{=}x_{\boldsymbol{k}})P(X_{\boldsymbol{j}}{=}x_{\boldsymbol{j}}{\mid}X_{\boldsymbol{k}}{=}x_{\boldsymbol{k}})
.
The pair
(X,{\cal M})
defines what is known as a
Markov network
.

Independence beyond distance
d

Let
{\cal G}=({\cal V},{\cal E})
be a
graph where the nodes in
{\cal V}
have (random) states
X
. We define
{\cal G}^{d}
as the graph in
which all nodes of
{\cal G}
are neighbours if they are at most a shortest distance
d

away from each other, i.e.

{\cal G}^{d}{=}({\cal V},{\cal E}^{d}),\;\;\mathrm{with}\;\;{\cal E}^{d}{=}\{(i,j){\in}{\cal V}:\mathrm{dist}_{\cal G}(i,j){\leq}d\}\mbox{.}

(16)

We then say that
({\cal G},X)
has
independence beyond distance
d
if
{\cal G}^{d}
is the independence map of
X
, or for all distinct subsets of nodes
\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j},\boldsymbol{k}\subset{\cal V}
holds

\boldsymbol{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}_{\mkern-4.0mu{\cal G}^{d}}\;\boldsymbol{j}\mid\boldsymbol{k}\implies X_{\boldsymbol{i}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}X_{\boldsymbol{j}}\mid X_{\boldsymbol{k}}.

(17)

This means that states of two non-neighbouring sets in
{\cal G}^{d}
, which by definition
(
16
) are further than
d
steps apart in
{\cal G}
, are independent of each
other given the state of their separator
\boldsymbol{k}
.

Maximal
d
-cliques and
d
-clique graph

A maximal clique is a complete subgraph not contained in a larger complete subgraph
Harary and Palmer 1973
. As a generalisation,
maximal

d
-cliques
are maximal subgraphs with distance between any two nodes
not greater than
d

Wolfram Research, Inc. 2021
. Correspondingly, maximal cliques in
{\cal G}^{d}
are maximal
d
-cliques in
{\cal G}
. The graph
{\cal C}^{d}
is the

d
-clique graph
of
{\cal G}
if
each node in
{\cal C}^{d}
corresponds to a
d
-clique in
{\cal G}

with links between nodes in
{\cal C}^{d}
occurring when the corresponding
d
-cliques
overlap. Hence, while nodes in
{\cal C}^{d}
correspond to maximal
d
-cliques in

{\cal G}
, links in
{\cal C}^{d}
correspond to intersections between overlapping
maximal
d
-cliques in
{\cal G}
.

Junction graph of
d
-cliques

A junction graph of

{\cal G}
’s maximal
d
-cliques, denoted further as
{\cal J}^{d}({\cal G})
, is a subgraph of the
d
-clique graph
{\cal C}^{d}
obtained by
removing redundant links from
{\cal C}^{d}
. Denoting
d
-cliques
corresponding to nodes
i,j
in
{\cal C}^{d}
as
c_{i},c_{j}\subset{\cal V}
, a link between

i
and
j
in
{\cal C}^{d}
is
redundant
when there is an alternative path between
i

and
j
in
{\cal C}^{d}
passing by a series of other nodes in
{\cal C}^{d}
of which the corresponding

d
-cliques all contain
c_{i}\cap c_{j}
. The junction graph
{\cal J}^{d}({\cal G})
is then obtained
by iteratively removing redundant links from the
{\cal C}^{d}
until there are no further redundant links. While the
d
-clique
graph is unique, there may be several junction graphs
{\cal J}^{d}({\cal G})
of
d
-cliques for one graph
{\cal G}
.
Note that for chordal graphs (defined below), the junction graph equals what is known as a junction
tree, which can also be obtained via the junction tree algorithm
Barber 2012
,
applied to the
d
-clique graph.

d
-chordality

A graph
{\cal G}
is chordal when for every cycle of length greater than
3
, there exists a link in
{\cal G}
between two non-consecutive nodes of the cycle (thus, giving a short-cut, also called
chord
to the cycle). As a generalisation, we will call a graph
{\cal G}

d
-chordal
if
{\cal G}^{d}
is chordal. If a graph
{\cal G}
is
d
-chordal, then

{\cal J}^{d}({\cal G})
is a tree, or equivalently, if
{\cal G}^{d}
is chordal,
then
{\cal J}({\cal G}^{d})
is a tree. Non-chordal graphs
can always be converted to a chordal graph via
triangulation
,
i.e. adding chords to every chordless cycle of length greater than
3
.
We will write below
\text{tr}({\cal G}^{d})
as a
minimal triangulation
of
a non-chordal
{\cal G}^{d}
, obtained by adding the smallest number of links
that leads to chordality, unless stated otherwise.

Decomposability at distance
d

If there is independence
beyond distance
d
(
17
) and
{\cal G}
is
d
-chordal,
then the joint probability of the network nodes of
{\cal G}
being in a
given state
P(X=\boldsymbol{x})
can be factorised over the

d
-cliques of
{\cal G}
. We will call this property of the graph

{\cal G}
and its node states
X

decomposability at distance

d
. We call
{\cal J}
the set of

d
-cliques in
{\cal G}
, ordering its elements consistent with the
resulting junction tree structure for
{\cal G}^{d}
(such that

{\cal J}_{1}
is the chosen root and parent nodes have lower index
than their leaves), and call
\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{i})
the parent node of

{\cal J}_{i}
. In such cases, the factorisation is possible because
the tree structure between
d
-cliques allows application of the chain
rule of conditional probability.

\displaystyle P(X{=}\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{|{\cal J}|}P(X_{{\cal J}_{i}}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{i}}\mid X_{\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{i})}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{i})}),

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{|{\cal J}|}P(X_{{\cal J}_{i}}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{i}}\mid X_{\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{i})\cap{\cal J}_{i}}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{i})\cap{\cal J}_{i}}),

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{|{\cal J}|}P(X_{{\cal J}_{i}}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{i}})}{\prod_{i=2}^{|{\cal J}|}P(X_{\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{i})\cap{\cal J}_{i}}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{i})\cap{\cal J}_{i}})}.

(18)

The steps in (
B
) are explained as follows. As
d
-chordality
makes
{\cal J}^{d}({\cal G})
a tree and any
d
-clique
separates its neighbours, one can recursively use conditional independence of children given parents (line 1). We use the convention that
\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{1})=\emptyset
, such that the first factor is
P(X_{{\cal J}_{1}}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{1}})
. In line 2 we exploit that any two
d
-cliques in

{\cal G}
are also separated by their intersection to condition instead on intersections.

Non-
d
-chordal graphs

There are two alternative ways to decompose non-
d
-chordal
{\cal G}
: (
i
) perform the decomposition (
B
) on the (more conservative) independence map after triangulation,
\text{tr}({\cal G}^{d})
.
In this case, the factors in (
B
) may still contain subgraphs of diameter
d{+}1
, but of smaller size than
{\cal G}
, such that one may have to apply (
B
) recursively to achieve smaller diameter for all factors. Furthermore, the resulting decomposition will depend on the choice of triangulation.
Alternatively, (
ii
), one can
start from the non-tree
{\cal J}^{d}({\cal G})
and use the ad-hoc formula (without prior triangulation)

P(X{=}\boldsymbol{x})\approx\zeta\frac{\prod_{i}^{|{\cal J}|}P(X_{{\cal J}_{i}}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{i}})}{\prod_{i,j\neq i}^{|{\cal J}|}P(X_{{\cal J}_{i}\cap{\cal J}_{j}}{=}\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{i}\cap{\cal J}_{j}})}.

(19)

Because the fraction in (
19
) does not result from
application of the chain rule as in (
B
), it is not a
product of conditional probabilities and hence it does not guarantee
the property that each of the node states in
\boldsymbol{x}
has to
appear one more time in the numerator than in the denominator, which
in turn leads to inconsistency between closure formulas that assume
different
d
. The factor
\zeta
in (
19
)
corrects for this inconsistency – see Section
C
for more
detail. After applying (
i
) to non-chordal graphs, the
nodes in the resulting
d
-clique tree are not all maximal

d
-cliques of
{\cal G}
any more
1
1

1

More precisely, they
consist of the nodes of the maximal cliques of

\text{tr}({\cal G}^{d})
and the links of
{\cal G}
.
.
When applying
(
ii
) to non-chordal graphs, the subgraphs in

{\cal J}^{d}({\cal G})
are still maximal
d
-cliques of
{\cal G}
but
the clique graph is not a tree, thus, violating the assumptions
behind the decomposition (
B
).

Maximum motif diameter

The decomposition explained in this
section implies that if independence beyond distance
d
is valid
in the whole network
({\cal G},X)
, then we know that we
only need to consider motifs up to diameter
d
, justifying truncation
of the moment hierarchy. Note, however, that truncation is usually
done at a give size, not at a given diameter. We expand on this
in point
C
.

C
Motif decomposition

Decomposition of motifs at the individual level

We
apply the decomposition (
B
) to motifs with
connectivity
\mathsf{a}
and chordal independence map

\mathsf{a}^{d}
embedded in the network. For now, we ignore that this
may in some cases break the independence assumption, but see section

C
for more detail on this issue. Considering a set

\boldsymbol{i}
of nodes that have connectivity
\mathsf{a}
in
our network
{\cal G}
and taking

P(X_{\boldsymbol{i}}{=}\boldsymbol{x})
as the probability that these
are in states with labels
\boldsymbol{x}
, we can write the
decomposition (
B
) for of
P(X_{\boldsymbol{i}}{=}\boldsymbol{x})=\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\rangle

to obtain

\displaystyle\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\rangle=\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{|{\cal J}|}\langle[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{{\cal J}_{j}}}]\rangle}{\prod_{j=2}^{|{\cal J}|}\langle[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{j})\cap{\cal J}_{j}}}]\rangle},

(20)

where now
{\cal J}
is the set of
d
-cliques in
\mathsf{a}
. Choosing

d=\text{diam}(\mathsf{a})-1
ensures that the decomposition results in component motifs with
diameter decreased by one compared to the decomposed motif. For motifs with non-chordal
\mathsf{a}^{d}
one
can, as noted above, either triangulate
\mathsf{a}^{d}
first
or use the
ad-hoc
approximation (
19
).
We relied on the package
Chordal Graph

Bulatov 2011
for
triangulation of non-chordal
\mathsf{a}^{d}
. The
ad-hoc formula (
19
) applied to the motif at
\boldsymbol{i}
is

\langle[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}]\rangle\approx\frac{\prod_{j}^{|{\cal J}|}\langle[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{{\cal J}_{j}}}]\rangle}{\prod_{j,k\neq j}^{|{\cal J}|}\langle[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{{\cal J}_{j}\cap{\cal J}_{k}}}]\rangle}\prod_{j\in\boldsymbol{i}}\langle[x_{j}]\rangle^{\textstyle\gamma_{j}}.

(21)

The consistency correction (written as
\zeta
in (
19
)) here equals
\prod_{j\in\boldsymbol{i}}\langle\left[x_{j}\right]\rangle^{\gamma_{j}}

and ensures that the ad-hoc extension of closures to motifs with non-chordal
\mathsf{a}^{d}

does not result in inconsistency with MF
1

(condition 1 of
Dieckmann et al. 2000
, Ch. 21)

under independence between node states: when
all motifs of order greater than one are replaced by products of order
one motifs, i.e.

\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}_{\cdot}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\rangle\to\prod_{j\in\boldsymbol{i}_{\cdot}}\langle\left[x_{j}\right]\rangle
, the right hand side of (
21
) should reduce to MF
1
. Therefore, for each
j
,
\gamma_{j}
is chosen such that this is fulfilled. These ad-hoc steps usually result in violation of the
conservation relations of Section
V

Dieckmann et al. 2000
. In
the approximations used in Section
VIII
, the bias introduced due to this violation is small. For mitigation of this problem, see

Dieckmann et al. 2000
;
Peyrard et al. 2008
.

Decomposition of motifs at the population level

If we take the following
spatial homogeneity
assumption for all motifs
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
of sizes
m
up to our maximal considered size
k

\forall\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i^{\prime}}\in{\cal I}(\mathsf{a}):\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\rangle=\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i^{\prime}}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\rangle=\frac{\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\rangle}{\left[\mathsf{a}\right]}=\langle\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\rrbracket\rangle,

(22)

where
{\cal I}(\mathsf{a}):=\{\boldsymbol{j}\in S(m,N):\mathsf{A}_{\boldsymbol{j}}=\mathsf{a}\}
,
then (
20
,
21
) are independent of
\boldsymbol{i}
, such that
we can write (
20
) as

\displaystyle\langle\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\rrbracket\rangle\approx\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{|{\cal J}|}\langle\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{j}}\rrbracket\rangle}{\prod_{j=2}^{|{\cal J}|}\langle\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathrm{pa}({\cal J}_{j})\cap{\cal J}_{j}}\rrbracket\rangle},

(23)

and (
21
) as

\langle\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\rrbracket\rangle\approx\frac{\prod_{j}^{|{\cal J}|}\langle\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{j}}\rrbracket\rangle}{\prod_{j,k}^{|{\cal J}|}\langle\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}_{{\cal J}_{j}\cap{\cal J}_{k}}\rrbracket\rangle}\prod_{p}^{m}\langle\llbracket x_{p}\rrbracket\rangle^{\textstyle\gamma_{p}},

(24)

which may be used to close the population-level equations (
10
).
In (
23
,
24
), node indexing of a motif is consistent with the node labels
\boldsymbol{x}
. Recall that we use a single consistent indexing for isomorphic motifs. The decomposition (
23
,
24
) is not unique. If the independence assumptions are satisfied each of the alternative ways to decompose motif
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
should result in the same value. As we do not expect the independence to be perfectly valid, we
take the average of the alternative ways of decomposing
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
if they exist.

Normalisation

We showed the closure formulas for
normalised motifs, i.e. we first normalised the counts of motifs
via (
8
), and then applied the closure. Hence, in this case, the counts
of induced subgraphs in the network enter into the system of equations as normalisation factors in the unclosed system. One can also decide not to normalise (or to do it after applying closure).
In this latter case, the subgraph counts
enter into the final system of equations when applying closure, as the
closure formulas for the non-normalised motif counts contain them
(to see this, substitute each motif count in (
23
,
24
)
as
\llbracket\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}\rrbracket\to[\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}]/[\mathsf{b}]
). Hence, structural information specific to the considered network enters the mean field equations either when normalising the motif counts or when applying closure. In simple cases,
such as lattices or random graphs,
the subgraph counts can be found by hand without much effort. In other cases, one can resort to
subgraph counting algorithms – we used
IGraph

Horvát 2021
for
Mathematica

Wolfram Research, Inc. 2021
.

Law of large numbers

We will use the population-level closure to study the
steady states in a single realisation of a given network. Motif counts are then assumed to be
the total counts in a single network, instead of their expectations over many
realisations. As the closure formulas apply to expectations, we make the additional assumption that motif counts are close to their expectations.

Additional bias

Decomposing
the whole network
{\cal G}
at distance
d

is exact when
{\cal G}
is
d
-chordal and there is independence beyond
distance
d
(Section
B
). Applying the decomposition to motifs
embedded in
{\cal G}
instead of to
{\cal G}
can be done without additional
bias when
\mathsf{a}

is a distance-hereditary subgraph of
{\cal G}
(i.e. distances between nodes
in
\mathsf{a}
are equal to those between corresponding nodes in
{\cal G}
) and
conditional independence relations implied by
\mathsf{a}
are also valid in
{\cal G}
.
As a counterexample for the former, take for

{\cal G}
the six-node graph

and for
\mathsf{a}
its induced subgraph consisting of nodes
\{1,2,4,6,5\}
. Here,
\mathsf{a}

is not distance-hereditary because
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{a}}(1,5){=}4\neq\mathrm{dist}_{\cal G}(1,5){=}2
.
As a counterexample for the latter, take for
{\cal G}
the square

and for
\mathsf{a}
the 3-node chain
\{1,2,4\}
. In this case,
\mathsf{a}
is distance hereditary, but, while (when assuming independence beyond distance
d{=}1
) within
\mathsf{a}
we have the independence relation
\{1\}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}_{\mkern-5.0mu\mathsf{a}}\;\{4\}\mid\{2\}
, this is not true in

{\cal G}
, where
\{1\}\not\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}_{\mkern-5.0mu{\cal G}}\;\{4\}\mid\{2\}
, because
the node cut set in
{\cal G}
for
\{1\}
and
\{4\}
is
\{2,3\}
. This occurs
because the decomposed motifs are non-maximal
d
-cliques. Therefore, we expect a bias as a consequence of this in the closed mean-field equation hierarchy
((
10
) with (
23
) or (
24
) at population level under spatial homogeneity). This bias can be avoided when
expressing the equations in terms of maximal
p
-cliques for
p\in\{0,...,k{+}1\}

and truncating at given diameter instead of at given size.

D
Examples

Appendix
A-VIII
shows
13
application examples of (
23
,
24
) in table form.
As the closures can be
written independent of the particular labels, they are shown for subgraphs only, with each node tagged with its index. We have
also dropped the
\langle\cdot\rangle
, assuming that the law of large numbers applies, such that the counts approach their expectations almost surely for increasing network size
N
. The examples can be understood
by reading the table from left to right.
Below, we derive
the normalisation factors and the non-normalised closures of examples 1-3 of
Appendix
A-VIII
for different network types. Note that, unlike in
Appendix
A-VIII
, we use letter labels below, for consistency with the main
text and the literature.

1.

\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc}}}
: This diameter-
2
motif has
chordal independence map equal to
\mathsf{a}
and decomposes with (
23
) as

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc}}}\rrbracket\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket},

(25)

assuming conditional independence beyond distance
d{=}1
.
Via normalization (
8
) we obtain also the closure for the non-normalized counts:

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc}}}\right]\approx\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc_nolab}}}\right]\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a_nolab}}}\right]}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2_nolab}}}\right]^{2}}\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\right]}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\right]}.

(26)

The counts of the induced subgraphs of size
2
and
1
, required for normalisation, are

\displaystyle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2_nolab}}}\right]=\kappa N,\quad\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a_nolab}}}\right]=N,

(27)

and total number of triples (
3
-node motifs) in the network is

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc_nolab}}}\right]+\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]=\sum_{i}^{N}\kappa_{i}(\kappa_{i}-1),

(28)

with
\kappa_{i}
the number of neighbours of node
i
and
\kappa
the mean number
of neighbours over the whole network. For particular network types (
28
) can be simplified. Below are two examples.

(a)

For a network with fixed degree without triangles (e.g. a square lattice), we have
\forall i:\kappa_{i}=\kappa
and
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]=0
,
such that

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc_nolab}}}\right]=\kappa(\kappa-1)N.

(29)

Using (
26
) and (
29
), we obtain

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc}}}\right]\approx\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\right]}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\right]}.

(30)

An early use of this closure for networks can be found in
Keeling et al. 1997
.

(b)

In a large Erdős-Rényi random network, we have
\kappa_{i}\sim\text{Pois}(\kappa)
and
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}]/([\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}]+[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc_nolab}}}])\approx 0

2
2

2

For an ER random network, the expected number of
m
-node cycles and
m
-node chains (counting all ordered
m
-tuples without repetition) are
L(N)=\frac{N!}{(N-m)!}(\frac{\kappa}{N-1})^{m}

and
C(N)=\frac{N!}{(N-m)!}(1-\frac{\kappa}{N-1})(\frac{\kappa}{N-1})^{m-1}
,
where
\frac{\kappa}{N-1}
is the probability of having a link between
two given nodes. Hence,
\mathrm{lim}_{N\to\infty}L(N)=\kappa^{m}=O(1)
and
\mathrm{lim}_{N\to\infty}C(N)=\mathrm{lim}_{N\to\infty}\kappa^{m-1}N=O(N)
. Hence, as
L(N)
remains finite and
C(N)
grows with
N
, cycles can be ignored in the limit of large
N
. In the case
m=3
(and
N
large), we can hence safely assume that all triples are chains.
. Hence

\displaystyle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc_nolab}}}\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}^{N}\kappa_{i}^{2}-\sum_{i}^{N}\kappa_{i},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N\left[\mathrm{E}(\kappa_{i}^{2})-\mathrm{E}(\kappa_{i})\right],

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N\left[\mathrm{Var}(\kappa_{i})+\mathrm{E}(\kappa_{i})^{2}-\mathrm{E}(\kappa_{i})\right],

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}N,

(31)

where replacing the average by the expectation on the second line requires
N\to\infty
(law of large numbers), on the third line we used
\mathrm{Var}(\cdot):=\mathrm{E}((\cdot)^{2})-(\mathrm{E}(\cdot))^{2}
, and on the fourth line we used that, for
\kappa_{i}\sim\text{Pois}(\kappa)
, we have
\mathrm{E}(\kappa_{i})=\mathrm{Var}(\kappa_{i})=\kappa
. We could also have obtained this result directly from the large-
N
limit of chains
Note1
.
Using (
26
) and (
31
), we obtain

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc}}}\right]\approx\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\right]}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\right]},

(32)

This closure was, to the best of our knowledge, first used for networks in
Gross et al. 2006
.

2.

\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr}}}
: This diameter-
1
motif
can be decomposed into its three
0
-cliques as

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr}}}\rrbracket\approx\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{c}}}\rrbracket,

(33)

when assuming independence of nodes (
d{=}0
). With non-normalised counts, this becomes

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr}}}\rrbracket\approx\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{c}}}\right]/\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a_nolab}}}\right]^{3}.

(34)

Alternatively, one can extend the usage of the ad-hoc formula (
24
)
to include non-maximal cliques: using its
three
1
-cliques in (
24
), we obtain

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr}}}\rrbracket\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ca}}}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{c}}}\rrbracket},

(35)

which is known as the Kirkwood closure for triangles
Sharkey 2008
. Using (
8
), we
obtain for the closure with non-normalised counts

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr}}}\right]\approx\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a_nolab}}}\right]^{3}}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2_nolab}}}\right]^{3}}\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ca}}}\right]}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{c}}}\right]}.

(36)

The frequency
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]
depends on
the network type. For instance, if we use the definition of the clustering
coefficient
\phi:=\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]/(\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]+\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc_nolab}}}\right])

Keeling 1999
, we have [via (
27
) and (
28
)] for a
network with fixed degree
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]=\phi\kappa(\kappa-1)N
, such that

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr}}}\right]\approx\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa^{2}}\phi N\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ca}}}\right]}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{c}}}\right]},

which was first used for networks by
Keeling 1999
.

3.

\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abcdtre}}}
: This diameter-
2
motif has
chordal independence map
\mathsf{a}
and decomposes with (
23
) as

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abcdtre}}}\rrbracket\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bd}}}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket^{2}},

(37)

when assuming independence beyond distance
d{=}1
.
Alternatively, extending the ad-hoc formula (
24
) to the three
non-maximal
3
-cliques, we obtain

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abcdtre}}}\rrbracket\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abc}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abd}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{cbd}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bc}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{bd}}}\rrbracket},

(38)

where a consistency correction
\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket
was required. The non-normalised form of this closure was first used in
House et al. 2009
.

VIII
Application to SIS epidemic spreading

We apply our method to SIS spreading, which is a
continuous-time discrete-state Markov chain description of epidemic
spreading through a population of susceptibles
(
Kiss et al. 2017
, e.g.)
. As introduced in Section
II
, we have
n=2
species. The
2\times 2
matrix
\mathbf{R}^{0}
of
spontaneous conversion rates and the
2\times 2\times 2
tensor
\mathbf{R}^{1}
of
conversion rates due to nearest-neighbour interaction for SIS
spreading have only two positive entries,
R^{0}_{2,1}{=}\gamma
,
R^{1}_{1,2,2}{=}\beta
, corresponding to reaction scheme (
2
).
Hence, contagion of susceptibles
S
occurs over
IS
links
at rate
\beta
, whereas recovery occurs spontaneously at rate
\gamma
.
In the study of phase transitions and interacting particle systems,
SIS epidemic spreading is known as the contact process
Harris 1974
,
which is typically studied on
\mathsf{d}
-dimensional lattices.
In this context, it was found to belong to the directed percolation
universality class, of which scaling properties have been widely studied

Marro and Dickman 1999
;
Henkel et al. 2008
;
Tomé and De
Oliveira 2015
.

We run the simulations with a Gillespie algorithm
Gillespie 2007
and stabilise them
via feedback control
Sieber et al. 2008
;
Schilder et al. 2015
;
Barton 2017
,
such that steady states can be obtained in a
more efficient manner than when running regular simulations (see
Appendix
A-III
).

A
Mean-field equations

We derived the mean-field models up to fifth order for the square lattice
and up to second order for other networks, including cubic/hypercubic lattices,
random regular networks and Erdős-Rényi random networks. In the main text,
we only show a step-by-step
derivation of the first and second-order mean-field models because they allow
demonstration of our method in the simplest form. Recall that we write in the text
\langle\left[\cdot\right]\rangle
as
\left[\cdot\right]
, assuming the LLN holds (in the
Mathematica
file for MF4 in
A-VII
, we use the notation

\langle\cdot\rangle
instead).

To gain insight in the strength of dependence between neighbouring nodes in simulations and higher-order mean-field models, we will observe the correlation between neighbouring node states
a
and
b
as in
Keeling 1999
, defined by

C_{ab}=\frac{N}{\kappa}\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\right]}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\right]},

(39)

(where
\kappa
is the mean degree) or when motif counts are normalised,

C_{ab}=\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ab2}}}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket}.

(40)

They are uncentered correlations between species types that are separated
by one link. Values greater than 1 indicate clustering and values
less than 1 avoidance (compared to a uniform random distribution). For
a generalisation of this correlation to arbitrary distances between
end nodes, see Appendix
A-IV
.

A.1
MF1

The first-order mean field originates from the molecular field approximation
in statistical physics
Weiss 1907
;
Bragg and Williams 1934
and is now commonly known
as the ‘mean field model’
Marro and Dickman 1999
;
Henkel et al. 2008
;
Tomé and De
Oliveira 2015
;
Porter and Gleeson 2016
;
Kiss et al. 2017
;
Newman 2018
.
MF1 only considers node states (
k=1
) and neglects
correlations beyond distance 0. It provides a picture of the dynamics when species
are well mixed throughout a large domain. One way to achieve this is when the domain
is a complete network on which susceptibles and infecteds have contact rate

\kappa\beta/N
, and when
N\to\infty

Kiss et al. 2017
.

There are two
motif types of size 1:

and

. Of these, only

is dynamically
relevant. This means that we only need the equation:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]=\beta[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]-\gamma[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}].

To close the system at order 1,
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]

needs to be expressed in terms of
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]
and
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right]
.
No correlation beyond distance 0 corresponds to (using (
23
)):

\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]}{\kappa N}=\frac{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]}{N}\frac{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]}{N}\implies\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]=\frac{\kappa}{N}[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}](N-\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]),

(41)

where we have also used the conservation relation
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right]+\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]=N

to substitute
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right]=N-\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]
.
The final expression for the first-order mean field is

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]=\beta\frac{\kappa}{N}[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}](N-\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right])-\gamma[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}],

which, after normalisation [via (
8
)], yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket=\beta\kappa\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket(1-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket)-\gamma\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket.

(42)

The steady state solutions are then

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket_{1}^{*}=0,\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket_{2}^{*}=1-\frac{\gamma}{\kappa\beta}.

At
\beta/\gamma=\kappa^{-1}
, the solution

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket_{1}^{*}
becomes unstable due to a transcritical
bifurcation, also known as the epidemic threshold in epidemiology.

A.2
MF2

The second-order mean field originates from the Bethe approximation
in statistical physics
Bethe 1935
and is now commonly known
as the ‘pair approximation’
Porter and Gleeson 2016
;
Kiss et al. 2017
;
Newman 2018
;
Matsuda et al. 1992
;
Keeling et al. 1997
;
Rand 1999
;
Dieckmann et al. 2000
;
Kéfi et al. 2007
;
Gross et al. 2006
.
MF2 neglects dependence beyond distance 1
and is obtained by considering all dynamically relevant motifs up to size 2. Noting
that motifs without infecteds are dynamically irrelevant and omitting zero blocks,
we obtain

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}&&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSItr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIItr}}}]\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]}&&-\gamma&\beta&0&&&&\\
\cline{3-9}\cr\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]}&&&-\beta-\gamma&\gamma&\beta&-\beta&\beta&-\beta\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]}&&&2\beta&-2\gamma&0&2\beta&0&2\beta\\
\end{array}\;.

Hence, two types of order 3 motifs appear on the right-hand side: chains and triangles.
For the networks we consider in this paper, triangular subgraphs are either not present
(in case of square, cubic, hypercubic lattices)
or negligible for large
N
(e.g. for Erdős-Rényi random networks
Note1
), so
we only need to consider the system

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}&&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}]\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]}&&-\gamma&\beta&0&&\\
\cline{3-7}\cr\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]}&&&-\beta-\gamma&\gamma&\beta&-\beta\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]}&&&2\beta&-2\gamma&0&2\beta\\
\end{array}\;.

The number of conservation relations used for elimination depends on whether the networks
have a homogeneous degree.

Networks with homogeneous degree

In this case,
the conservation relations are

\displaystyle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]+\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right]=

\displaystyle N,

(43)

\displaystyle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]+\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}\right]=

\displaystyle\kappa\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right],

(44)

\displaystyle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\right]+\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]=

\displaystyle\kappa\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right],

(45)

However, due to the dynamic irrelevance of
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]
and
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}]
, only (
45
)
can be used to eliminate further variables. We use it to eliminate
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\right]=\kappa[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]-[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]
. Following (
11
-
13
),
this means

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]\\
\end{bmatrix}^{T},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]\\
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]\\
\end{bmatrix},\;\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{3}=\begin{bmatrix}[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]\\
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}]\\
\end{bmatrix},

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\kappa&-1\\
\end{bmatrix},\;\mathbf{c}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix},\;\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1\cdots 2,1\cdots 2}=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-\gamma&\beta&0\\
0&-\beta-\gamma&\gamma\\
\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{23}=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\beta&-\beta\\
\end{bmatrix},

such that we can calculate
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathbf{c}}
to obtain

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}&&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}]\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]}&&-\gamma&\beta&&\\
\cline{3-6}\cr\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]}&&\gamma\kappa&-\beta-2\gamma&\beta&-\beta\\
\end{array}\;.

(46)

Applying
the closure (
30
) for degree-homogeneous networks [resulting from (
23
)],

\displaystyle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\kappa(\kappa-1)N\frac{\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}\right]}{\kappa N}\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]}{\kappa N}}{\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right]}{N}}=\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\frac{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}][\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]}{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]},

\displaystyle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\kappa(\kappa-1)N\frac{\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]^{2}}{\kappa^{2}N^{2}}}{\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right]}{N}}=\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\frac{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]^{2}}{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]},

(47)

we obtain the final nonlinear system

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}&&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]&\frac{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}][\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]}{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]}&\frac{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]^{2}}{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]}\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]}&&-\gamma&\beta&&\\
\cline{3-6}\cr\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]}&&\gamma\kappa&-\beta-2\gamma&\beta\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}&-\beta\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\\
\end{array}\;,

(48)

which, after elimination of
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]
and
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}]
via (
43
-
44
)
and normalisation via (
8
) becomes

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket&\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket&(1-\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket}{1-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket})\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket&\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{2}}{1-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket}\\
\dot{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket}&-\gamma&\kappa\beta&&\\
\cline{2-5}\cr\dot{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket}&\gamma&-\beta-2\gamma&\beta(\kappa-1)&-\beta(\kappa-1)\\
\end{array},

(49)

From the steady state solutions (
A33
)
we find that the epidemic threshold,
is now located at
\beta/\gamma{=}(\kappa-1)^{-1}
. We also derived non-trivial steady state correlations via (
40
) in (
A34
).

Networks with heterogeneous degree

Here, (
44
)
and (
45
) do not hold, but the total frequency of any given subgraph is still conserved. Hence, the conservation relations are (
43
) from order
1
and

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}\right]+\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\right]+2\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]=\kappa N.

(50)

This means that we cannot eliminate
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]
here (unlike in case of networks
with homogeneous degree), such that we have three instead of two equations.
Also applying the closure for ER networks (
32
) [resulting from (
23
)],

\displaystyle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}\right]\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right]},\qquad\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}\right]\approx\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]^{2}}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\right]},

(51)

we obtain

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}&&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]&\frac{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}][\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]}{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]}&\frac{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]^{2}}{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]}\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]}&&-\gamma&\beta&0&&\\
\cline{3-7}\cr\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]}&&&-\beta-\gamma&\gamma&\beta&-\beta\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]}&&&2\beta&-2\gamma&0&2\beta\\
\end{array}\;.

(52)

After substitution of
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}]
and
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}]

via the conservation relations (
43
,
50
) and normalisation via (
8
), this becomes

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}&\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket&\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket&\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\rrbracket&\frac{(1-2\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\rrbracket)\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket}{1-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket}&\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{2}}{1-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket}\\
\dot{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket}&-\gamma&\kappa\beta&0&&\\
\cline{2-6}\cr\dot{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket}&&-\beta-\gamma&\gamma&\kappa\beta&-\kappa\beta\\
\dot{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\rrbracket}&&2\beta&-2\gamma&0&2\kappa\beta\\
\end{array}\;.

(53)

There are three steady states, of which two are in the admissible range (
A35
).
Here, the epidemic threshold is located at
\beta/\gamma{=}\kappa^{-1}
, as in the first-order
mean field model. The steady state correlations are given in (
A36
).

A.3
MF3-MF5

Approximations of higher order than two correspond to cluster variation
approximations in statistical physics
Kikuchi 1951
.
MF3 results from neglecting dependence
beyond distance 2 and is commonly known as the ‘triple approximation’
House et al. 2009
.
Its step-by-step derivation for the square lattice is shown in Appendix
A-VI

and results in four equations (after substitution with conservation relations).
In Appendix
A-VII
, we show the derivation of the unclosed
MF4 for a general network as
Mathematica
notebook output. We
also derived the closed MF4 and MF5 for the square lattice. The number of equations after
elimination with conservation relations is respectively 14 and 37. The derivation
of MF4 with closure is shown in Appendix
A-VII
point 2.
The steady states of
MF4 and MF5 are shown in Figure
2
of Section
B
.

B
Comparison to simulations

Here, we compare the steady states of MF1-5 of
SIS epidemic spreading with those of the simulations on a selection of
network types: lattices, regular random networks and Erdős-Rényi random
networks.

Square lattice

In Figure
2
a,
we show, for the square lattice, the steady state fraction of infecteds versus

\beta/\gamma
for MF1-MF5 compared to simulations. The steady states of the mean-field
models get closer to those of the simulation with increasing order. All mean-field
models have an increasing bias in their non-trivial (endemic) steady states when approaching the critical value of
\beta/\gamma
from above. Figure
2
b compares
the steady state distance-1 correlations between species types from MF2 and MF5
to those in simulations. Species of the same type cluster whereas
different species tend to avoid each other (compared to a random distribution).
The infected-infected correlation diverges when approaching the critical value of
\beta/\gamma
from above and has a singularity
at the bifurcation. E.g. for MF
2
, via (
A34
), we have
C_{II}^{*}(\beta/\gamma)\to\infty
for
\beta/\gamma\,\searrow\,(\kappa-1)^{-1}

(limit from above in the endemic equilibrium). We recall that the errors in the mean-field models visible in Figure
2
a can be due to violation of the statistical dependence assumption beyond distance
k
, non-chordality of
the independence map, and violation of the spatial homogeneity assumption.
Spatial homogeneity can be violated in two ways: via heterogeneity of structure
and via heterogeneity of dynamics
Sharkey 2008
. As the structure
of a lattice is homogeneous and we only study steady states (i.e.
there are no dynamics) that are spatially homogeneous (see Figure
A2
b), spatial inhomogeneity can not be a source of bias here.
This leaves statistical dependence and non-chordality
as only sources of bias. In the square lattice there are always cycles with diameter
larger than
\mathsf{d}
for any chosen
\mathsf{d}
along which unaccounted for information can spread, unless
\mathsf{d}
is greater than or equal to the graph diameter of the entire lattice. This means that
mean-field models that do not consider motifs of size up to the network
diameter minus one cannot be exact.

As MF1
assumes no correlation between
the states of neighbouring nodes, the distance between the horizontal line
through
1
and the
\boldsymbol{+}
markers of the correlations in the simulations
is a measure of the bias of MF1 due to neglection of correlations
in MF1 closures. Likewise, the distance between the steady state MF2/MF5
correlations and the simulations is due to neglection of (higher-order
and conditional) dependence in MF2/MF5 closures and higher-level non-chordality.
All models have larger biases closer to the critical point, where higher-order correlations become more important. This is a well-known characteristic of continuous phase transitions, in
which correlations occur on increasingly long ranges when approaching
the phase transition (bifurcation). Figure
A3
in Appendix
A-IV
shows the correlation as a function of distance from a central point
[via (
A30
)], for various values of
\beta/\gamma
. It shows,
as expected from phase transitions theory, that correlations at any
distance are larger closer to the critical point. At the critical
point, theory shows that correlations occur at all distances
Henkel et al. 2008
.

Comparing the square lattice to the 4-neighbour random regular graph, we can see that
there is also a phase transition, but there is substantially less bias than in the square
lattice (Figure
3
blue
\boldsymbol{\times}
vs
\boldsymbol{+}
). This is because random regular graphs are
locally treelike, and hence, unlike in the square lattice, correlations over longer
distances can be captured well via a decomposition of larger motifs into links (MF2).
The small remaining bias in the 4-regular random graph we suspect to be
because the assumed conditional independence is not valid for all states

Sharkey et al. 2015
;
Sharkey and Wilkinson 2015
.

Figure 2:
Comparison of the mean-field approximations of order
1
to
5
(lines) with simulations (markers)
of SIS epidemic spreading on a square lattice as a function of
\beta/\gamma
:
(a) Nontrivial steady states, (b) Correlations at distance
1
[shown for
MF
2
(solid), MF
5
(dashed), and simulations (dots)].

General cubic lattices and random regular networks

We show in Figure
3
how the steady
states and correlations in MF1, MF2 and simulations depend on the number
of neighbours in
\mathsf{d}
-dimensional cubic lattices and random regular networks. When

\mathsf{d}
is
the lattice dimension, the lattice degree is
\kappa=2\mathsf{d}
. The observations
of the square lattice generalise to cubic/hypercubic lattices and
random regular networks (at least up to
\kappa=10
):
i
. there is
a transcritical bifurcation at a particular value of
\beta/\gamma
,
where
C_{II}^{*}
becomes singular,
ii
. MF1 and MF2 capture qualitatively
the steady state fraction of infecteds and correlations are captured
qualitatively by MF2,
iii
. MF2 is less biased than MF1,
vi
. the
bias is larger closer to the bifurcation. According to MF1, the bifurcation
occurs at
\kappa^{-1}=(2\mathsf{d})^{-1}
and according to MF2 at

(2\mathsf{d}-1)^{-1}=(\kappa-1)^{-1}

(see Sections
A.1
and
A.2
).

Liggett 2005
proved that for lattices, the critical value
predicted by MF2 is a lower bound. Figure
3
a
shows that MF2 and this lower bound is approached increasingly closely
when the lattice dimension increases. Due to higher clustering of
neighbours, the epidemic threshold in lattices is higher than that
in the corresponding random regular network
Keeling 1999
,
but this difference decreases with dimension/degree (Figure
3
a).
The steady states of a 5-dimensional hypercubic lattice and of a random
regular network with degree 10 are indistinguishable from each other
and from MF2. This is because random walks in space of dimension 5 or higher
have a finite number of intersections almost surely

Erdős and Taylor 1960
;
Heydenreich and van der
Hofstad 2017
;
Lawler and Limic 2010
. If
the path along which an infection travels is seen
as a random walk, having many intersections in
\mathsf{d}\leq 4
means that
one cannot ignore alternative infection paths. In
\mathsf{d}>4
, it is harder
for infections to travel via alternative paths to the same point,
and hence those paths resemble trees more closely. Hence, as explained above
and in
Sharkey and Wilkinson 2015
, MF2 should then be more accurate.

Figure 3:
Comparison of the mean-field approximations (dotted lines: MF1, solid
lines: MF2) and simulations (markers) of SIS epidemic
spreading on lattices and random regular networks with number of neighbours
4,6,8,10 as a function of
\beta/\gamma
: (a) Nontrivial steady states,
(b) Correlations:
C_{II}^{*}
(above 1),
C_{SI}^{*}
(below 1).

Erdős-Rényi random networks

Finally, we show in Figure
4
how the
steady states and correlations in MF1, MF2 and simulations depend on
the number of neighbours in an Erdős-Rényi random network. Recall
that MF2 on an Erdős-Rényi random network is different from that of the networks
above because it has one fewer conservation relation. As above, the
behaviour of the steady state solutions is captured qualitatively
by MF1 and MF2 and of the correlations by MF2 alone. Also as above,
networks with a larger degree have lower biases and MF2 is better
than MF1, but now there seems to be a slight increase of bias with
\beta/\gamma
,
at least in the range inspected. Despite spatial heterogeneity
of the degree in Erdős-Rényi random networks, there is considerably less bias
than in lattices, confirming that the presence of cycles beyond closure distance is the dominant cause for mean-field model biases in the steady states of SIS spreading.

Figure 4:
Comparison of the mean-field approximations (dotted lines: MF1, solid
lines: MF2) and simulations (markers) of SIS epidemic spreading
on an Erdős-Rényi random network with number of neighbours 4,6,8,10
as a function of
\beta/\gamma
: (a) Nontrivial steady states, (b)
Correlations:
C_{II}^{*}
(above 1),
C_{SI}^{*}
(below 1).

IX
Summary and conclusions

Previous work found that exact closed individual-level
moment equations exist for SIR spreading on arbitrary networks,
with the requirement to consider larger-sized motifs, and therefore more equations,
for networks that are decreasingly tree-like
Sharkey and Wilkinson 2015
.
While for other dynamics than SIR spreading it may not be possible to
prove exactness for a finite number of closed moment equations,
it is generally found that accuracy
increases with the order of approximation
(
House et al. 2009
;
Kiss et al. 2017
, e.g.)
(which was confirmed here). Feasibility of automated derivation of exact closed moment equations
for SIR epidemic spreading was shown by
Sharkey and Wilkinson 2015
, while an automated procedure
to derive unclosed moment equations for arbitrary dynamics was developed by
Danos et al. 2020
.
We developed an automated procedure to both derive and close population-level
moment equations for arbitrary dynamics on networks at any approximation order, allowing us to consider mean-field models of higher orders than typically derived by hand.
For this purpose, we developed a method to derive closure schemes from predefined
independence assumptions. Our closure formulas rely, besides the requirements of spatial homogeneity and
large network size, on the assumption of conditional independence beyond
distance
k
and
k
-chordality of the considered network.
Consistently, our simulations of SIS epidemic spreading showed
that, at given approximation order, the largest biases occurred for networks with many short cycles of any size, such as lattices, in parameter regimes with long-range correlations, such as near continuous phase transitions. Note however that, for lattices, we found the bias of mean-field models to decrease with lattice dimension, which is consistent with results in percolation and phase transitions theory,
where it was shown that the importance of cycles decreases with the lattice dimension

Erdős and Taylor 1960
;
Heydenreich and van der
Hofstad 2017
;
Lawler and Limic 2010
. We also showed
that the conventional procedure of truncation at a maximum motif size instead
of at a maximum motif diameter necessitates independence assumptions
that are inconsistent for different approximated motifs or that may be incompatible with the network (see
C
). This suggests that choosing a moment space
that consists of motifs at increasing diameter instead of increasing size
would lead to more accurate mean-field models.

Whereas our method still needs to be tested more widely, we expect it to lend
itself well to study dynamics on networks
with density and size of short cycles between that of random networks
and (low-dimensional) lattices, particularly when
the network is structurally homogeneous. In these cases,
derivation by hand may be too tedious while the final
set of moment equations is still more manageable than
the Markov chain simulations.
For networks with considerable
degree heterogeneity
and/or community structure, we expect
approximate master equation methods

Marceau et al. 2010
;
Gleeson 2013
;
Fennell and Gleeson 2019
;
St-Onge et al. 2021
;
Cui et al. 2022
to be more efficient.
Our
approach focused on static networks with at most
nearest-neighbour interactions, but it can be extended to adaptive
networks such as those studied in

Gross et al. 2006
;
Demirel et al. 2014
;
Danos et al. 2020
, and to dynamics
with higher-order interactions
Battiston et al. 2020
– requiring
reaction rate tensors
\mathbf{R}^{p}
with
p\geq 2
. Our approximation
scheme of Section
VII
can be applied more generally,
to understand precisely which independence assumptions are taken in
other existing types of mean-field
approximations than moment closure, or to devise new mean-field
approximations.
It may also serve to extend the use of message-passing methods

Karrer and Newman 2010
;
Wilkinson and Sharkey 2014
;
Koher et al. 2019

for epidemic modelling to graphs with cycles.

While we were able to derive
closure formulas by assuming statistical independence,
leading to mean-field models, other assumptions can be used
to obtain closures
Kuehn 2016
,
such as maximum entropy
Rogers 2011
, or time scale separation
between moments at different orders
Gross and Kevrekidis 2008
.
Sometimes one can find an appropriate moment space and closure by taking account of the characteristic
features of the process in consideration, leading to a description with greater efficiency compared to what is obtainable by using the size-based moment space and closing via independence assumptions
Wuyts and Sieber 2022
.
We expect that for finding good moment spaces and closures, equation-free and machine-learning
methods
Kevrekidis and Samaey 2009
;
Patsatzis et al. 2022
will
play an important role, in particular because the most appropriate low-dimensional descriptions or their closures may not necessarily be available in closed form

Rogers 2011
;
Gross and Kevrekidis 2008
;
Kevrekidis and Samaey 2009
;
Patsatzis et al. 2022
. It is subject of future work to explore if and how these different approaches relate.

Acknowledgements.

This work was supported by the UK Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council (EPSRC) grants EP/N023544/1 and EP/V04687X/1.

APPENDIX

Notation and references

References to items in this document are preceded
by ‘A’. Items not preceded by ‘A’ refer to the main text.

A-I
Derivation of the moment equations

In this section, we derive
expressions for the expected rate of change and conservation relations
of motif counts, first shown in (
8
,
9
) of Section

V
. Both can be seen as invariance
relations, the former being of a differential type, and derived from the master equation for the Markov chain on the network, and the latter of an
algebraic type, following from the property that the network is fixed.

Master equation for transitions in a Markov chain

We
recall that the state of the system for our network with
N
nodes is
given by (using
:=
for “defined as”)

X:=(X_{1},X_{2},...,X_{N})\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{N},

where
X_{i}
is the label of the species that occupies node
i
. Hence, the total number of states is
n^{N}
.
The probabilistic transition from one state to another, following a discrete-state continuous-time Markov chain, defines an evolution equation for the probability of being in each of these states, the so-called
master equation (also known as the Kolmogorov-forward
equation for a Markov jump process
Gardiner 2009
). The probability density
P(X,t)
for a particular
state
X
at time
t
changes according to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}P(X,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{X^{\prime}\neq X}\left[w(X^{\prime}\rightarrow X)P(X^{\prime},t)-w(X\rightarrow X^{\prime})P(X,t)\right],

(A1)

where
w
denotes the transition rate between system states. If we
define
\mathbf{W}
as a
n^{N}\times n^{N}
transition rate matrix
with non-diagonal entries
w(X^{\prime}\rightarrow X)
and diagonal entries

-\sum_{X^{\prime}\neq X}w(X\rightarrow X^{\prime})
, we can rewrite the master equation
as

\dot{\mathbf{\mathbf{P}}}(t)=\mathbf{W}\mathbf{P}(t),

which describes the evolution of the density for all states as elements of a vector
\mathbf{P}

(and not just of one particular state as in (
A1
)). Because
almost surely at most one node can change state at any one time
t
, only states differing from each other in one node can be directly
transitioned between. Therefore,
\mathbf{W}
must be sparse, having
only
N(n-1)
entries in each row or column, as each of the
N

nodes can convert to any of the
n-1
other species. This permits
writing (
A1
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}P(X,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k\neq X_{i}}^{n}\left[w_{i}(X_{i\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mkern 2.0mu\hbox{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 0 L 0 6.96 L 7.08 6.96 L 7.08 0 Z} {{{}{}{{}}{}

(A2)

where

X\to X_{i\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mkern 2.0mu\hbox{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 0 L 0 6.96 L 7.08 6.96 L 7.08 0 Z} {{{}{}{{}}{}
