is the operator that
replaces the species at the
i
th node by species
k
, and
w_{i}(.)

is the conversion rate at node
i
.

Motifs and their frequencies

In what follows, we will derive from the master equation the evolution
of the frequency (or total count) of the
motifs

\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
, as defined in Section
II
. Recall that these motifs,
are defined by their state label vector
\boldsymbol{x}
of size
m
and the
adjacency structure between motif nodes
\mathsf{a}
. We denote a single
occurrence
at a given
\boldsymbol{i}\in S(m,N)
as

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right](t)

\displaystyle:=\delta_{\mathsf{a}}(\mathsf{A}_{\boldsymbol{i}})\delta_{\boldsymbol{x}}(X_{\boldsymbol{i}}(t))\mbox{,}

(A3)

which requires an
exact match of the adjacency
\mathsf{a}
by

\mathsf{A}_{\boldsymbol{i}}
and state label vector
\boldsymbol{x}
by
X_{\boldsymbol{i}}
.
For example, if
\mathsf{A}
contains a
connected triangle between nodes
1
,
2
and
3
, then motifs with

\mathsf{a}=\{(1,2),(2,3)\}
would not occur on

\boldsymbol{i}=(1,2,3)
. The total count of motifs in the large network is then the number of such exact matches, which is obtained by summing over all indices
\boldsymbol{i}\in S(m,N)
, as shown in (
3
).
Since the large network structure is constant in time, we can use the counts
of induced subgraphs
\left[\mathsf{a}\right]
given by (
8
) as a normalisation factor for the (variable) counts of motifs such that we may consider normalised motif frequencies

\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\rrbracket:=\left[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]/\left[\mathsf{a}\right]\mbox{.}

(A4)

Evolution of expected counts

Total and normalised counts

\left[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]
and

\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\rrbracket
refer to realisations of states
X
on the large random network such
that they are random variables. Similarly,

\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right](X)
is a
random variable in
\{0,1\}
for each index vector
\boldsymbol{i}\in S(m,N)

once we take the randomness of states
X
into account. Its
expectation is

\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\rangle=\sum_{X}\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right](X)P(X,t)\mbox{.}

(A5)

The master equation (
A2
) for the density
P
implies that the expectation satisfies the differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{X}\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right](X)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}P(X,t)

(A6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{X}\Bigl\{\delta_{\mathsf{a}}(\mathsf{A}_{\boldsymbol{i}})\delta_{\boldsymbol{x}}(X_{\boldsymbol{i}})\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\sum_{k\neq X_{i^{\prime}}}^{n}\left[w_{i^{\prime}}(X_{i^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mkern 2.0mu\hbox{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 0 L 0 6.96 L 7.08 6.96 L 7.08 0 Z} {{{}{}{{}}{}

to denote
a vector with element
p
removed. The expression on the
right-hand side in (
A6
) can be understood independent of
the prior algebraic manipulations: the expected change rate of

\bigl\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\bigr\rangle

equals the sum of expected rates for each of the nodes
i_{p}
in

\boldsymbol{i}
changing its state
to

x_{p}
minus the rate of
node
i_{p}
changing its state
from

x_{p}
.

Conversion rates

Next, we will insert the two types of admissible transitions
(as discussed in Section
II
) into
(
A6
), namely spontaneous conversions with rates given in
matrix
\mathbf{R}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and,
conversions due to interactions with a
single nearest neighbour with rates given in

\mathbf{R}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
. The diagonal entries of
\mathbf{R}^{0},\mathbf{R}^{1}
are zero without loss of generality. For these transition types, the rates
w_{i_{p}}

A6
) are

\displaystyle\left.-\sum_{c}^{n}R^{1}_{x_{p}kc}\Bigl\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\sum_{j}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{i_{p}j}\delta_{c}(X_{j})\Bigr\rangle-R^{0}_{x_{p}k}\Bigl\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\Bigr\rangle\right].

This shows that
\bigl\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\bigr\rangle

can increase by a conversion from motifs that differ from
\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}

in only one node (first two terms), or decrease by having any of the
nodes in
\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}
convert to another species
(last two terms), with both increase and decrease possible via interaction
with neighbours and via spontaneous conversion. The factors of form

\left[\cdot\right]\sum_{j}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{i_{p}j}\delta_{c}(X_{j})
count the number of

c
-connections of motif
[\cdot]
(located at
\boldsymbol{i}
) at node
i_{p}
.
The neighbouring node
j
with species
c
can be part of the motif
[\cdot]
, or it can be outside of
[\cdot]
, in which case it gives rise to higher-order motifs. Therefore, by splitting
the neighbourhood sums as follows,

\displaystyle\sum_{j}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{i_{p}j}\delta_{c}(X_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\boldsymbol{i}}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{i_{p}j}\delta_{c}(X_{j})+\sum_{j\notin\boldsymbol{i}}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{i_{p}j}\delta_{c}(X_{j}),

(A8)

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\sum_{j}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{i_{p}j}\delta_{c}(X_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\delta}_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathsf{a}\boldsymbol{e}_{p}\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]+\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}\in{\cal N}^{c}_{p}\left({\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}}\right)}\sum_{j\notin\boldsymbol{i}}^{N}\left[\boldsymbol{y}_{\boldsymbol{i},j}^{\mathsf{b}}\right]\mbox{.}

(A9)

On the right-hand side,
\boldsymbol{e}_{p}
is an
m
-dimensional vector with a
1
at position
p
and zeroes elsewhere, and
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})
a vector Kronecker delta function that returns a vector of the size of
\boldsymbol{x}
with ones where the elements equal
c
and zeroes elsewhere. Thus, the term
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathsf{a}\boldsymbol{e}_{p}
counts the number of
c
-connections at position
p
in the motif
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
.
We use the notation

\boldsymbol{i},j
for
the vector
\boldsymbol{i}
with an extra
node index
j
appended
at position
m+1
. We used
{\cal N}^{c}_{p}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}})
to denote the set of all
(m+1)
th order connected motifs that can be obtained by linking a new
c
-node to the
p
th position in motif
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
, i.e.,

{\cal N}_{p}^{c}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}):=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m+1}\left\{\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}:|\boldsymbol{y}|=m+1,y_{\ell}=c,\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}_{\ell\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mkern 2.0mu\hbox{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 0 L 0 6.96 L 7.08 6.96 L 7.08 0 Z} {{{}{}{{}}{}

denotes the
m
th order connected motif obtained by deleting the
\ell
th node of
\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}
. The sum over elements of
{\cal N}^{c}_{p}(\cdot)
in (
A9
) is taken because any of the other motif nodes can also link to the new node. The types of
higher-order motifs appearing in the differential equation depend
on the considered motif. In Figure
A1
,
we show this dependence structure (ignoring the labels).

Dependence of moment equations of a given motif on motifs of one order higher due to nearest-neighbour interactions, up to order 4 (ignoring labels).

substitutions from above, we obtain the

individual-level moment equations
:

\displaystyle-\Bigl(\sum_{c}^{n}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathsf{a}\boldsymbol{e}_{p}R^{1}_{x_{p}kc}+R^{0}_{x_{p}k}\Bigr)\bigl\langle\left[\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{i}}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]\bigr\rangle-\sum_{c}^{n}\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}\in{\cal N}^{c}_{p}\left({\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}}\right)}\sum_{j\notin\boldsymbol{i}}^{N}R^{1}_{x_{p}kc}\bigl\langle\left[\boldsymbol{y}_{\boldsymbol{i},j}^{\mathsf{b}}\right]\bigr\rangle\biggr\}.

where we collected the sums at the front.
In closing this section, we make the following remarks.
i
. The evolution of expected motif counts of size
m
is a function of expected motif counts of size
m
and
m+1
.
ii
. Equation (
A12
) leads to the same equation for motifs in the network that are
isomorphic
, where we call two motifs
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}
isomorphic if there exists a permutation
\pi
of their node indices that maps them onto each other, i.e.
\forall\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}},\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}:\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\simeq\boldsymbol{y}^{\mathsf{b}}\iff\exists\pi:\pi\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{y},\,\pi\mathsf{a}\pi^{T}=\mathsf{b}
. Hence, different motifs that are isomorphic belong to the same equivalence class. Isomorphic motifs have equal total counts such that we may consider only one representative from each equivalence class. In our implementation, we therefore make sure that we count all isomorphic motifs under a single representative node indexing.
iii
. The summation in (
3
) over index tuples
\boldsymbol{i}\in S(m,N)
that we used to go from (
A11
) to (
A12
) leads to multiple counting of motifs that possess
automorphisms
other than the identity transformation. A motif has an automorphism if it can be mapped onto itself by a permutation of its node indices. An automorphism is therefore an isomorphism with itself, i.e.
\mathrm{Aut}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}):=\{\pi:\pi\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{x},\,\pi\mathsf{a}\pi^{T}=\mathsf{a}\}
. The multiplicity with which a particular motif
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
is counted (via (
3
)) is then equal to
|\mathrm{Aut}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}})|
.

Conservation relations

In fixed networks, the frequencies of induced subgraphs of a given
type (e.g. nodes, links, polygons, chains) remain fixed. The sum over all
possible motif label orderings then yields these fixed frequencies, or using
the notation from above,

\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\right]=\left[\mathsf{a}\right],\qquad\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}}\llbracket\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}\rrbracket=1,

(A13)

where the right is the normalised form of the left [via
(
A4
)]. For networks with homogeneous degree and motifs and every stub
p
of motif
\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
(also called leaf), i.e. a node with degree
1
, there is the additional conservation relation

\sum_{k}\left[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}_{p\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mkern 2.0mu\hbox{\hbox to5.12pt{\vbox to5.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@discardpath@clipped{M 0 0 L 0 6.96 L 7.08 6.96 L 7.08 0 Z} {{{}{}{{}}{}

(A14)

where the right is again the normalised form
of the left (via (
A4
)).
Under the conditions mentioned above,
\left[\mathsf{a}\right]/\left[\mathsf{a}\setminus p\right]

is the number of out-motif connections at the node that connects to
the stub. As (
A13
) and (
A14
) can be derived for each motif up to the chosen
truncation order, they form an additional system of equations that can be used to
reduce the dimensionality of the mean-field model via elimination. To avoid multiplicity of
equations while deriving relations (
A14
), we set up one equation per set of stubs that
lead to isomorphic variants of the considered subgraph when their indices are permuted.

Number of equations

A simple lower bound on the number of equations (before elimination)
can be found by considering only chains. At each order
there is only one chain graph. Order 1 contributes
n
equations, where
n
is the number of possible node states. Each chain
of order
m>1
has
\left(\binom{n}{m}\right)=\binom{m+n-1}{n}=\frac{(m+n-1)!}{(m-1)!n!}

ways of labelling its
m
nodes with
n
species. The total number
of equations from chains is then
n_{\mathrm{ch}}=2+\sum_{m=2}^{k}\left(\binom{n}{m}\right).

For networks in which the number of short cycles goes to zero with

N\rightarrow\infty
, as in Erdős-Rényi random networks, the total
number of equations is equal to this lower bound plus the number of equations due to non-chain trees. When cycles need
to be taken into account however, there will be additional
connected motif types at each order; see Table
A2
column
n_{g}

(
Harary and Palmer 1973
, Table 4.2.1)
.
To obtain the number of equations contributed by each of these, one
has to consider all labelling orderings, knowing that some orderings
lead to isomorphic motifs and hence do not add to the total. We have listed in Table
A2
the total number
of equations
n_{\mathrm{eq}}
resulting from our enumeration algorithm
for dynamics with
n=2
, such as SIS epidemic spreading.
For a particular network, these are still reduced by
the number of motifs not occurring in the considered network and by
the number of variables via the conservation relations. Column

n_{4}
in Table
A2
shows the number of equations for the
square lattice and column
n_{4c}
shows the remaining number after eliminating
variables by using conservation relations.

k

n_{\mathrm{ch}}

n_{g}

n_{\mathrm{eq}}

n_{\mathrm{4}}

n_{\mathrm{4c}}

Cumulative number of equations (
n_{\mathrm{ch}}
,
n_{\mathrm{eq}}
,
n_{\mathrm{4c}}
) or motif
types (
n_{g}
) as a function of order
k
.
n_{\mathrm{ch}}
: number of chain motifs (if the number of species
n=2
),
n_{g}
: number of subgraph types,
n_{\mathrm{eq}}
: total number of equations ignoring conservation relations (if
n=2
),
n_{\mathrm{4}}
: number of equations for the square lattice (if
n=2
),
n_{\mathrm{4c}}
: number of equations for the square lattice after elimination via conservation relations (if
n=2
).

A-II
Moment equations for SIS spreading up to order 3

The moment equations up to third order can be written (via (
10
)) as

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}&\mathbf{x}_{1}&\mathbf{x}_{2}&\mathbf{x}_{3}&\mathbf{x}_{4}\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{1}&\mathbf{Q}_{1}&\mathbf{Q}_{12}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{2}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{Q}_{2}&\mathbf{Q}_{23}&\mathbf{0}\\
\dot{\mathbf{x}}_{3}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{Q}_{3}&\mathbf{Q}_{34}\\
\end{array}

(A15)

Then, the coefficients for motifs up to order three are (omitting zero blocks)

{\tiny\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccc}&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SII}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{III}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSItr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIItr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IIItr}}}]\\

\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]}&&2\beta&-2\gamma&0&0&0&2\beta&0&0&2\beta&0\\
\cline{2-12}\cr\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIS}}}]}&&&&-2\beta-\gamma&0&2\gamma&0&0&0&0&0\\

\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SII}}}]}&&&&\beta&\beta&-\beta-2\gamma&0&\gamma&0&0&0\\
\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}]}&&&&0&0&0&-2\beta-2\gamma&\gamma&0&0&0\\

\dot{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IIItr}}}]}&&&&0&0&0&0&0&0&6\beta&-3\gamma\\
\end{array},}

while those for fourth-order motifs in
\mathbf{Q}_{34}
are

{\tiny\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccccccccccccccccc}&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSSItre}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSSIsqo}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSSIstr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SISSsqi}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SISI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SISIsqo}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SISIstr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SISIsqi}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSIItre}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSIIsqo}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISSIstr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSIIstr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IISSsqi}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IISI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIIIsqo}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IISIstr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IISIsqi}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISIItre}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISIIstr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IIISsqi}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISSSstr}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISSSsqi}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSSIsqii}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISISsqi}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSIIsqii}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIIIsqii}}}]\\

\end{array}}.

When written out, this corresponds to the equations:

(A26)

A-III
Obtaining steady states from simulations

Feedback control

As we aim to compare the steady states
of mean-field models to those of the simulation, we need a way to obtain the steady
states of the simulations, even if they are unstable or marginally stable.
Treating the simulation like an ideal physical experiment, the general approach to finding equilibria regardless of stability is to introduce a stabilizing feedback loop of the form

r(t)=r_{0}+g([\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}](t)-[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}]_{\mathrm{ref}})\mbox{,}

(A27)

as was done in

Sieber et al. 2008
;
Schilder et al. 2015
;
Barton 2017
for
continuation of unstable vibrations in mechanical experiments. In
(
A27
),
r
is one of the conversion rates in
\mathbf{R}^{0}
or
\mathbf{R}^{1}
.
The feedback control
3
3

3

(
A27
) is
the simplest possible form of feedback control as it changes only
one input (
r
) depending on one output (
[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}]
)
and is static (no further processing
of
[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}]
before feeding it back). Linear
control theory ensures that unstable equilibria of ODEs can be
stabilized with single-input-single-output dynamic feedback control
using any single input and any single output satisfying some
genericity conditions (linear controllability and
observability).
makes
this rate time dependent by coupling it to the motif frequency

[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}](t)
of a chosen motif

\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}
through the relation
(
A27
). The factor
g
is called the
feedback control gain and is problem specific.
When performing bifurcation analysis, it is convenient if the rate

r
used as the control input is also the bifurcation parameter
varied for the bifurcation diagram. In this case, whenever the
simulation with feedback control (
A27
) settles to an
equilibrium

(r_{\mathrm{c}}^{*},[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}]_{\mathrm{c}}^{*})
(in
the limit of large
N
) the point

(r_{\mathrm{c}}^{*},[\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}]_{\mathrm{c}}^{*})
will
be on the equilibrium branch of the simulation without feedback control
Barton and Sieber 2013
;
Sieber et al. 2014
;
Renson et al. 2017
.
For SIS spreading, we choose

r=\beta\mbox{,}\quad\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathsf{a}}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\mbox{,}\quad g=\infty\mbox{.}

(A28)

This limit for feedback control results in what is called the conserved contact process, as proposed by
Tomé and De
Oliveira 2001
. While SIS spreading does not have any unstable steady states,
points that are marginally stable, as near the
continuous phase transition (epidemic threshold) are stabilised as well by the control. This stabilisation suppresses fluctuations,
even close to the bifurcation, which results in faster convergence of the mean
and absence of absorption for any positive
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]
.

Figure A2:
Feedback control of SIS epidemic spreading on a square lattice:
(a) example time profiles of infected fraction
[I]
and
fraction of susceptible-infected links

[SI]
in conventional (left) and controlled (right) simulation,
(b) bifurcation diagram with control;
insets: example spatial patterns;
grid size 100
\times
100 (white=susceptible, black=infected), gain

g\gg 1
, recovery rate
\gamma=1
. Near the critical point,
the conventional simulation shows large fluctuations and short
extinction times (absorption event occurs at the end
of the time series for
\beta\approx 0.43
in green) whereas the
controlled simulation has small fluctuations and no extinction.

Simulation algorithm

In the limit of infinite gain
g
, each recovery event forces a simultaneous
infection event, such that the number of infected nodes
[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]
stays constant.
In a simulation based on the Gillespie algorithm
Gillespie 2007
this is
done in the following steps:

1.

start with a number of randomly distributed infected
nodes,

2.

recover an infected node selected uniformly at
random,

3.

infect a randomly selected susceptible node,
with selection probability proportional to its number of infected
neighbours,

4.

advance time with

\Delta t=-\log(\xi)/(\gamma\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right])
, (where
\xi
is a
uniform random variable on the interval
[0,1]
),

5.

go to step
2
.

This loop runs until we observe that
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right](t)
is stationary (call this time
t_{\mathrm{e}}
). Then for some additional time
T
we observe the fluctuations of
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right](t)
around its mean.
Tomé and De
Oliveira 2001
derived the effective
infection rate
\tilde{\beta}
for each chosen count

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]
of infected nodes by noting that, because for every
infection event there is a recovery event,

\tilde{\beta}\langle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]\rangle=\gamma\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right],

where
\langle\cdot\rangle
is an average over many independent realisations, such that

\tilde{\beta}=\gamma\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]}{\langle\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right]\rangle},

which they found to lead to the same nontrivial steady states

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]^{*}(\tilde{\beta}/\gamma)
as
\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]^{*}(\beta/\gamma)

in the model without control.
As the model with control is ergodic (no absorbing states exist) we need to run only a single realisation and compute the effective infection rate as

\tilde{\beta}=\gamma\frac{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\right]}{\int_{t_{\mathrm{e}}}^{t_{\mathrm{e}}+T}{\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\right](t)\mathrm{d}t}/T}.

(A29)

In this manner, the error bars
of estimates of
\tilde{\beta}
can be made arbitrarily small by increasing
T
.

A-IV
Correlations at given distance

In Figure
A3
we show for SIS epidemic spreading the correlation at given distance between two nodes (only between infected nodes shown).
Its general definition is

C_{ab}^{D}=\frac{N^{2}}{\sum_{ij}\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{dist}(i,j)=D}}\frac{\sum_{ij}[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}_{i}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}_{j}]_{\mathrm{dist}(i,j)=D}}{[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}][\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}]},

(A30)

or with normalised motifs:

C_{ab}^{D}=\frac{\sum_{ij}\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}_{i}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}_{j}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{dist}(i,j)=D}}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket},

(A31)

where the distance
\mathrm{dist}(i,j)
is the length of the shortest path between
i
and
j
. Note that the correlations between
neighbouring nodes (
39
) is a special case of this, i.e.

C_{ab}=C_{ab}^{1}
. An alternative way to write (
A31
) is

C_{ab}^{D}=\frac{\sum_{c_{1},...,c_{D-1}}\llbracket ac_{1}...c_{D-1}b\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{a}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{b}}}\rrbracket},

(A32)

where
\llbracket ac_{1}...c_{D-1}b\rrbracket
is a chain motif of size
D+1

with indicated states. This definition
was used to derive
C_{ab}^{D}
as approximated by mean-field
models in Figure
A3
, by applying the closure formula to the chain in the numerator.

Figure A3:
Steady state correlation function
C_{II}^{D}
versus distance
D

(
A30
) for simulations of SIS spreading on the square
lattice (lines). Colour
scale indicates values of
\beta/\gamma
corresponding to the markers
in Figure
2
and sorted such that higher correlations
occur for lower values of
\beta/\gamma
(closer to the critical
point). Markers show approximated correlations when assuming MF2 conditions
hold (using (
A32
)) and factorising the numerator in
pair fractions). Filled black markers are MF2 correlations for
\beta/\gamma=0.4137

(which for simulations is the top black line). Open red markers are
MF2 correlations for
\beta/\gamma=0.5602
(which for simulations
is the red line).

A-V
Steady states of MF1-MF2

Here, we show the expressions for the steady states and steady state correlations of MF1 and MF2.

A
MF1

The MF1 model equals SIS epidemic spreading under well-mixed conditions. Its steady states are the trivial and the endemic state,

\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket_{1}^{*}=0,\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket_{2}^{*}=1-\frac{\gamma}{\kappa\beta}.

B
MF2

For the MF2 model the equations and, hence, their steady states, depend on the type of network.

B.1
Degree-homogeneous networks

The steady states of the dynamic equations for MF2 on degree-homogeneous networks (
49
) are

(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket^{*},\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{*})_{1}=(0,0),\qquad(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket^{*},\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{*})_{2}=(1-\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa\beta(\kappa-1)/\gamma-1},\frac{\beta(\kappa-1)-1}{\beta(\beta\kappa(\kappa-1)-1)}).

(A33)

The non-trivial correlations, obtained by substitution of the above into (
40
), are

C_{II}^{*}=\frac{(\gamma-\beta\kappa)(\gamma-\beta(\kappa-1)\kappa)}{\beta\kappa^{2}(\beta(\kappa-1)-\gamma)},\quad C_{SI}^{*}=1-\frac{\gamma}{\beta\kappa(\kappa-1)},\quad C_{SS}^{*}=\frac{\beta(\kappa-1)\kappa-\gamma}{\beta(\kappa-1)^{2}}.

(A34)

B.2
Degree-heterogeneous networks

The steady states of the dynamic equations for MF2 on degree-heterogeneous networks (
53
) are

\displaystyle(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket^{*},\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\rrbracket^{*},\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{*})_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(0,0,0),

\displaystyle(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket^{*},\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\rrbracket^{*},\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{*})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma\left(\sqrt{\beta(\kappa-1)^{2}+4\gamma}-\sqrt{\beta}(\kappa+3)\right)}{2\beta^{3/2}\kappa}+1,

(A35)

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma\sqrt{\beta}(\kappa+1)-\gamma\sqrt{4\gamma+\beta(\kappa-1)^{2}}}{2\kappa\beta^{3/2}}).

The non-trivial correlations, via substitution of the above into (
40
), are

\displaystyle C_{II}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\beta^{3/2}\kappa-2\sqrt{\beta}\gamma(\kappa+3)+2\gamma\sqrt{\beta(\kappa-1)^{2}+4\gamma}}{\sqrt{\beta}\kappa\left(\sqrt{\beta(\kappa-1)^{2}+4\gamma}-\sqrt{\beta}(\kappa+1)\right)^{2}},

(A36)

\displaystyle C_{SI}^{*}

\displaystyle=C_{SS}^{*}=\frac{2\gamma}{\kappa\sqrt{\beta^{2}(\kappa-1)^{2}+4\beta\gamma}-\beta(\kappa-1)\kappa}.

(A37)

A-VI
MF
3
for SIS spreading

We only apply MF3 to the square lattice, such that we
can ignore all motifs that contain triangles and set

\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{abctr_nolab}}}\right]=\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{str}}}\right]=\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{sqi}}}\right]=\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{sqii}}}\right]=0

in the remaining equations, such that we obtain

{\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccccccccc}&&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIS}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SII}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{III}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSSItre}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSSIsqo}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SISI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SISIsqo}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISSI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSIItre}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSIIsqo}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IISI}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIIIsqo}}}]&[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISIItre}}}]\\

\end{array}.}

(A39)

To close the system of equations, we apply (
23
) to the chains and star, and (
24
) to
the cycles (using the extension to non-maximal 2-cliques) and obtain the normalised closures
(see also Section

, examples 3-5)

\displaystyle\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISSI}}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}\rrbracket^{2}}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}\rrbracket}=\frac{(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}\rrbracket)^{2}}{(1-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket)},

\displaystyle\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IISI}}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SII}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket}=\frac{(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{III}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket)\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket},

\displaystyle\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISIItre}}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{3}}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{S}}}\rrbracket^{2}}=\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{3}}{(1-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket)^{2}},

(A40)

\displaystyle\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSIIsqo}}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SII}}}\rrbracket^{2}\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SSI}}}\rrbracket^{2}}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{2}\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SS}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\rrbracket}=\frac{(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{III}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket)^{2}(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}\rrbracket)^{2}}{(1-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket)(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket)\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{2}},

\displaystyle\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SIIIsqo}}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{SII}}}\rrbracket^{2}\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{III}}}\rrbracket\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{2}\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{II}}}\rrbracket^{2}}=\frac{\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{III}}}\rrbracket(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{III}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket)^{2}\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{ISI}}}\rrbracket}{(\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{I}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket)^{2}\llbracket\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[inkscapelatex,height]{IS}}}\rrbracket^{2}},

where we also used the conservation relations to substitute any previously eliminated motif.
The steady state solutions of the final system
are roots of a ninth-order polynomial, of which two are admissible (see Figure
2
for numerical results).

A-VII
MF4 for SIS spreading in
Mathematica

We uploaded two application examples of our algorithm at fourth order to
Mathematica
’s
notebook archive
(best viewed locally).
In the
first section of the file, the procedure as explained in the main text is followed for MF4 on the square lattice.
The second section in the file shows the general coefficient matrix
\mathbf{Q}
for the (unclosed) moment equations up to fourth order, with columns labelled by
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{5}
(stored in the
variable
mots
).
The algorithm can be instructed to write the resulting set of equations as
MATLAB

MathWorks 2021
functions in a format compatible
with continuation software such as
COCO

Schilder et al. 2021
and

MatCont

Dhooge et al. 2003
.

A-VIII
Closure examples

Here we show 13 examples of subgraph decompositions based on the
method explained in Section
VII
(
23
,
24
). They are shown in table form
on the next page
but we also discussed three commonly used ones (1-3 in the table) in
Section
D
.

As the closures can be
written independent of the particular labels, they are shown in the table for subgraphs only, with each node tagged with its index. We have
also dropped the
\langle\cdot\rangle
, assuming that the law of large numbers applies, such that the counts approach their expectations almost surely for increasing network size
N
. The examples can be understood
by reading the table from left to right. Comments are added in the last column. By column, it shows: 1. the
example number, 2. the considered subgraph, 3. its diameter,
4. its independence map assuming independence beyond distance
\text{diam}(\mathsf{a}-1)
, 5. whether the independence map is chordal,
6. the derived junction graph of
d
-cliques where
d=\text{diam}(\mathsf{a}-1)
, 7. the resulting
closure formula, 8. a triangulation of the independence map if the independence map
is non-chordal, 9. the junction graph
of
d
-cliques based on the triangulation, 10. the closure formula based on the
triangulation, 11. an ad-hoc extension of the method to non-maximal cliques for some
subgraphs, 12. the resulting closure formula based on this extension, 13. comments.
Our
Mathematica
script
Wuyts 2022
generates these closures automatically.
